Notes on the Jellinek-Berry Thermostated Ideal Gas
Authors: Leo T. Butler, Alireza Sharifi
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
This note studies Hamiltonian systems which are thermostated using the Jellinek–Berry thermostat (J. Phys. Chem. 1988; Phys. Rev. A 1988). Jellinek & Jellinek and Berry propose an extension of Nosé’s thermostat (J. Chem. Phys. 1984). They introduce multiple functional parameters in order to achieve ergodicity of the thermostated dynamics. This family of Hamiltonian thermostats aims to simulate the canonical ensemble of a Hamiltonian H by coupling H to a 1-d heat reservoir with potential energy v(s) and kinetic energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dfrac{1}{2Q}(p_s/u(s))^2$$\end{document}12Q(ps/u(s))2. This note derives a normal form for the reservoir’s potential energy; investigates when the Jellinek–Berry thermostated system admits a Hoover reduction; and, demonstrates that a Jellinek–Berry thermostated periodic ideal gas is completely integrable and satisfies a KAM twist condition called Rüssmann non-degeneracy. This is used to deduce that a thermostated, collision-less, non-ideal gas (i.e. one with a smooth potential energy) at sufficiently high temperatures of the reservoir has a positive measure set of invariant tori–hence, the thermostated dynamics are non-ergodic.

Introduction

A key concern in molecular dynamics is the computation of thermodynamical properties of a statistical-mechanical system. The landmark paper by Nosé [14] demonstrates a way that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{{NVT}}}$$\end{document}NVT ensemble can be computed using an extended Hamiltonian. Recall that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{{NVT}}}$$\end{document}NVT ensemble assumes constancy of the number, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{N}}$$\end{document}N, of particles; the volume, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{V}}$$\end{document}V, of the system; and the temperature, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{T}}$$\end{document}T, of the heat bath with which the system is in thermal equilibrium.

The Nosé thermostat introduces an additional state variable, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s, with an associated potential energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$gkT \ln s$$\end{document}gkTlns and kinetic energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_s^2/2Q$$\end{document}ps2/2Q. The parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q is called the thermostat mass. Recall that the microcanonical ensemble of a Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document}F is determined by fixing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{N}}$$\end{document}N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{V}}$$\end{document}V and the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F=E$$\end{document}F=E instead of the temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{T}}$$\end{document}T. which is the expected value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi $$\end{document}ψ according the Gibbs–Boltzmann distribution, i.e. the canonical ensemble of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H in a heat bath with temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T.

Early studies and numerical investigations reveal that the Nosé thermostat is not effective in producing a Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document}F that is ergodic on fixed energy levels [15]. Legoll, Luskin and Moeckel subsequently prove, using KAM theory, that the Hamiltonian flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document}F is not ergodic on energy levels when the thermostat mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q is sufficiently large [12, 13]. Several Hamiltonian thermostats that extend Nosé’s are in the literature, e.g. [3]. The most general form in the literature that preserves the simplicity of Nosé’s thermostat, while introducing many additional functional parameters is due to Jellinek [9] and Jellinek & Berry [10].

The latter generalization is significant in many respects. For instance, the choice of coupling determines whether particular first integrals of the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H are inherited by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F$$\end{document}F: Nosé’s coupling implies that linear-in-momentum first integrals are inherited. With an extended range of possible couplings, one can study how different couplings affect the dynamics. Moreover, it is hoped that these couplings can be chosen to achieve desired dynamical properties, such as ergodicity or a specific energy distribution. It is also hoped that extending the range of possible thermostat potential functions will achieve similar goals.

The Jellinek–Berry Thermostat

The point of departure of the Jellinek–Berry thermostat is the momentum-rescaling coordinate change \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =p/s$$\end{document}ρ=p/s in (1) [9, 10]. Consider the simultaneous transformation of the configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q$$\end{document}q and momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p$$\end{document}p by invertible linear transformations that depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s; a rescaling of the thermostat momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_s$$\end{document}ps by a positive scalar \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/u(s)$$\end{document}1/u(s) and let the Nosé potential, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ln (s)$$\end{document}ln(s), be replaced by an arbitrary function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=v(s)$$\end{document}v=v(s). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=(q,p)$$\end{document}x=(q,p) be the state vector of the Hamiltonian. One arrives at a generalized Jellinek-Berry thermostated Hamiltonian (c.f. [9, eqn. 49]):4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G(x,s,p_{s}) := H(a(s) \mathbin {\cdot }x) + \dfrac{p_{s}^2}{2\, Q\, u(s)^2} + gkT v(s). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G(x,s,ps):=H(a(s)·x)+ps22Qu(s)2+gkTv(s).Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a$$\end{document}a is an invertible linear transformation that depends on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s. In the work of Jellinek & Jellinek–Berry it is assumed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(s)$$\end{document}a(s) is diagonal in the given canonical coordinates.

Let us note that rescaling the thermostat momentum, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_s$$\end{document}ps, by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/u(s)$$\end{document}1/u(s) is equivalent to rescaling the Nosé thermostat mass by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(s)^2$$\end{document}u(s)2. From this viewpoint, one derives the Hamiltonian (4) from the Nosé Hamiltonian (2) via the transformations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \mapsto a(s) \mathbin {\cdot }x, Q \mapsto {\mathscr {Q}}(s)$$\end{document}x↦a(s)·x,Q↦Q(s) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {Q}}(s)$$\end{document}Q(s) is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s-dependent thermostat mass,5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathscr {Q}}(s) = Q\, u(s)^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Q(s)=Qu(s)2.We prefer this viewpoint for two distinct reasons. The first reason is geometric in nature. The Nosé Hamiltonian (2) has a configuration space that can be described as the metric cone on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M$$\end{document}M minus the cone point.1 For example, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M$$\end{document}M is the unit circle, its metric cone is the Euclidean plane, the configuration space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G is the plane less the origin since the potential energy is negative infinity at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=0$$\end{document}s=0. On the other hand, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M$$\end{document}M is a Euclidean space and the thermostat momentum is rescaled by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(s)=s$$\end{document}u(s)=s, then the configuration space of the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G is the hyperbolic upper half-space with constant negative curvature. More broadly, when the coupling map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(s)$$\end{document}a(s) only acts on the momenta, the kinetic part of the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G can be described in geometric terms, since the effect of the transformations on momenta are equivalent to defining an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s-dependent (inverse) mass matrix. The second reason is really a consequence of the first: the main results of this paper are most naturally and easily stated in terms of the inverse mass matrices, see sections 3 and 4.

It is necessary to formulate hypotheses on the various functional parameters in the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G (4). The following are based on analogies with the Nosé thermostat.

Assumption 1

The potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v:{\textbf{R}}^{+} \rightarrow {\textbf{R}}$$\end{document}v:R+→R is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell +1}$$\end{document}Cℓ+1 diffeomorphism and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell > 2n+3$$\end{document}ℓ>2n+3 where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n =\dim M$$\end{document}n=dimM.

Assumption 2

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a:{\textbf{R}}^{+} \rightarrow GL({{\textbf{R}}^{2n}})$$\end{document}a:R+→GL(R2n) is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell +1}$$\end{document}Cℓ+1 map.

Assumption 3

The Nosé thermostat mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathscr {Q}}{} : {\textbf{R}}^{+} \rightarrow {\textbf{R}}^{+}$$\end{document}Q:R+→R+ is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ map.

Ideal Gas

An ideal gas has only kinetic energy, so when that kinetic energy is defined by a flat metric (as it generally is in molecular dynamics), the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H is completely integrable. In this case, as Posch, Hoover and Vesely note [15], the Nosé Hamiltonian is completely integrable.2 Holian, Voter and Ravelo [6] observe that the Nosé–Hoover equations can, when the thermostat mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q is small, drive a many-body Hamiltonian system to have “persistent non-local oscillations.” This observation is attributed to a Toda-like potential energy that is buried in the Nosé Hamiltonian. Indeed, this Toda-like potential energy is the potential energy of the Nosé Hamiltonian applied to an ideal gas. That is to say, the Toda-like demon of Holian, et. al. is the potential energy observed by Posch, et. al.. Similarly, the normal form that Legoll, et. al. derive in their study of the Nosé–Hoover thermostated harmonic oscillator has that same Toda-like potential energy (see eqn. 27 and the following in [13]).

Outline

This note is organized as follows. Section 2 demonstrates that Assumption 1 implies a simple normal form for the Jellinek–Berry thermostat potential. This is used to determine sufficient conditions that imply the Jellinek–Berry thermostat has a reduction to a Nosé–Hoover-like thermostat. It is also used to derive a two-parameter family of weight functions that generalizes Jellinek’s [9, eqn. 31] and permits one to fix the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g$$\end{document}g to be the number of degrees of freedom, as in the Nosé–Hoover thermostat. Section 3 studies the n-dimensional ideal periodic gas. We show in Theorem 2 that the Jellinek–Berry thermostated ideal gas Hamiltonian is completely integrable. And, under two additional assumptions on the coupling map between the system and heat bath (see Assumptions 4 and 5 in section 3.1), we show in Theorem 5 that the frequency map of the system satisfies a twist condition called Rüssmann non-degeneracy. It is also shown that “almost all” coupling maps produce a frequency map that is Kolmogorov non-degenerate. In section 4 these conditions are used to prove that, even when a sufficiently smooth potential energy is added, the thermostated system is not ergodic on fixed energy levels when the temperature of the heat bath is sufficiently high (see Theorem 8). That is the key result of this note. This note concludes in section 5.

Normal forms

A normal form provides a reference point for a mathematical object. This section shows that the Assumption 1 implies that the thermostatic potential energy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v$$\end{document}v, can be transformed by a change of variables to either Nosé’s form (a natural logarithm)[14] or to that of Jellinek and Dettmann & Morriss [4, 9] (the identity). It also shows how, under several conditions, the transformation of the “virtual” state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = (q,p)$$\end{document}x=(q,p) to a “physical” state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y = a(s) \mathbin {\cdot }x$$\end{document}y=a(s)·x, when combined with a time reparameterization, can be used to reduce the Jellinek-Berry thermostated Hamiltonian vector field to the form of a Nosé–Hoover vector field.

Universality of the Nosé potential

To remove some arbitrary functional parameters, let’s investigate an implication of Assumption 1. By this Assumption, there is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ canonical change of coordinates6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma&= e^{v(s)},&p_{\sigma }&= p_{s}/\left( v'(s) e^{v(s)} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ=ev(s),pσ=ps/v′(s)ev(s),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v'(s)$$\end{document}v′(s) is the derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(s)$$\end{document}v(s) with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s. The transformed thermostated Hamiltonian takes the form7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G(x,\sigma ,p_{\sigma }) := H(b(\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }x) + \dfrac{p_{\sigma }^{2}}{2R(\sigma )} + gkT \ln (\sigma ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G(x,σ,pσ):=H(b(σ)·x)+pσ22R(σ)+gkTln(σ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(\sigma ) = {\mathscr {Q}}(s)/(v'(s) e^{v(s)})^{2}$$\end{document}R(σ)=Q(s)/(v′(s)ev(s))2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\sigma )=a(s)$$\end{document}b(σ)=a(s). That is, Assumption 1 implies that the Nosé potential can be assumed as given.

The linear thermostat potential

An alternative to Nosé’s logarithmic thermostat potential is a linear potential. Jellinek discusses the linear thermostat potential in the framework of time changes of Hamiltonian vector fields (see [9, eqn.s 61–64] and related discussion). In this work, the dynamics are designed to reproduce the canonical ensemble and they preserve a Hamiltonian, but the dynamics are non-Hamiltonian, in general. Dettmann & Morriss [4] use the linear thermostat potential in their study of the Nosé–Hoover thermostat, where they show that the Nosé–Hoover equations can be realized using a Hamiltonian formalism. From our point of view, a linear thermostat potential offers computational advantages when analyzing normal forms and non-degeneracy conditions like the Rüssmann and Kolmogorov non-degeneracy conditions (see section 3).

By Assumption 1, there is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ canonical change of coordinates8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nu&= v(s),&p_{\nu }&= p_{s}/v'(s). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ν=v(s),pν=ps/v′(s).The transformed thermostated Hamiltonian takes the form9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G(x,\nu ,p_{\nu }) := H(b(\nu ) \mathbin {\cdot }x) + \dfrac{p_{\nu }^2}{2R(\nu )} + gkT \nu , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G(x,ν,pν):=H(b(ν)·x)+pν22R(ν)+gkTν,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(\nu ) = {\mathscr {Q}}(s)/(v'(s))^2$$\end{document}R(ν)=Q(s)/(v′(s))2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\nu )=a(s)$$\end{document}b(ν)=a(s). That is, Assumption 1 implies that this linear potential can be assumed as given.

In the remainder of this note, Assumption 1 is taken to hold throughout, and the linear thermostated Hamiltonian of the form (9) shall be considered from section 3 onward.

The Physical transformation

In this and subsequent subsections, we return to the form of the Jellinek–Berry thermostat with Nosé’s logarithmic potential. We do this so that the calculations in subsection 2.6 closely mirror the derivations of the Nosé–Hoover thermostat equations from the Nosé thermostat [7]. The standard interpretation of the canonical coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=(q,p)$$\end{document}x=(q,p) is that they are virtual. The physical coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y$$\end{document}y are related to the virtual coordinates by the transformation:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y = a(s) \mathbin {\cdot }x = b(\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y=a(s)·x=b(σ)·x.Let’s call this the physical transformation. This is consistent with the terminology in the literature on the Nosé–Hoover thermostat, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p$$\end{document}p is regarded as a “virtual” momentum and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =p/s$$\end{document}ρ=p/s is regarded as a “real” or “physical” momentum.

The Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G (7), and its associated Hamiltonian vector-field, are transformed to: 11a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{G}(y,\sigma ,p_{\sigma })&= H(y) + \dfrac{p_{\sigma }^2}{2R(\sigma )} + gkT \ln (\sigma ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G^(y,σ,pσ)=H(y)+pσ22R(σ)+gkTln(σ),11b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{y}&= b(\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }J \mathbin {\cdot }b(\sigma )^* \mathbin {\cdot }\textrm{d}{H}(y) - \dfrac{p_{\sigma }}{R(\sigma )} \varphi (\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y˙=b(σ)·J·b(σ)∗·dH(y)-pσR(σ)φ(σ)·y,11c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\sigma }&= \dfrac{p_{\sigma }}{R(\sigma )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ˙=pσR(σ),11d\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{p}_{\sigma }&= \dfrac{R'(\sigma )}{2 R(\sigma )^2} p_{\sigma }^2 + \langle {d{H}(y)},{\varphi (\sigma )\mathbin {\cdot }y}\rangle - gkT/\sigma , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p˙σ=R′(σ)2R(σ)2pσ2+⟨dH(y),φ(σ)·y⟩-gkT/σ,11e\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathrm {where\ } \varphi (\sigma )&= -b'(\sigma )\mathbin {\cdot }b(\sigma )^{-1} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}whereφ(σ)=-b′(σ)·b(σ)-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^*$$\end{document}b∗ is the transpose of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b$$\end{document}b, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J$$\end{document}J is the symplectic matrix and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\cdot },{\cdot }\rangle $$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩ is the inner-product, or pairing, of a vector and a co-vector. The reader should note that the sign on the friction-like second term in the right-hand side of (11b) is correct, due to the sign on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (\sigma )$$\end{document}φ(σ). Indeed, in subsection 2.6.1, we show that these equations reproduce the Nosé–Hoover thermostat equations under the appropriate assumptions on the scaling matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\sigma )$$\end{document}b(σ).

Since the physical transformation is not necessarily symplectic, the vector field is not canonical; but, it does preserve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{G}$$\end{document}G^. Since the volume form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}{x} \, \textrm{d}{s} \, \textrm{d}{p_s} $$\end{document}dxdsdps is invariant for the Hamiltonian vector field of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G (4), the transformed volume form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}{y} \, \textrm{d}{\sigma } \, \textrm{d}{p_{\sigma }} \times (\det b(\sigma ))^{-1}$$\end{document}dydσdpσ×(detb(σ))-1 is invariant for this vector field in physical coordinates.

The independence transformation

A final, non-canonical, coordinate transformation transforms the total energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{G}$$\end{document}G^ (11a) into a sum of three independent terms: 12a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \zeta&= p_{\sigma }/\sqrt{R(\sigma )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ζ=pσ/R(σ),12b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widehat{G}(y,\sigma ,\zeta )&= H(y) + \frac{1}{2} \zeta ^{2} + gkT \ln (\sigma ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G^(y,σ,ζ)=H(y)+12ζ2+gkTln(σ), and the vector field to: 13a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{y}&= b(\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }J \mathbin {\cdot }b(\sigma )^* \mathbin {\cdot }\textrm{d}{H}(y) - \dfrac{\zeta }{\sqrt{R(\sigma )}} \varphi (\sigma )\mathbin {\cdot }y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y˙=b(σ)·J·b(σ)∗·dH(y)-ζR(σ)φ(σ)·y,13b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\sigma }&= \dfrac{\zeta }{\sqrt{R(\sigma )}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ˙=ζR(σ),13c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\zeta }&= \dfrac{1}{\sigma \,\sqrt{R(\sigma )}} \left( \langle {\textrm{d}{H}(y)},{\sigma \,\varphi (\sigma )\mathbin {\cdot }y}\rangle - gkT \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ζ˙=1σR(σ)⟨dH(y),σφ(σ)·y⟩-gkT. The volume form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}{y}\, \textrm{d}{\sigma }\, \textrm{d}{\zeta } \times \sqrt{R(\sigma )}/\det (b(\sigma ))$$\end{document}dydσdζ×R(σ)/det(b(σ)) is invariant for this vector field.

Let’s note some similarities of these equations with the Nosé–Hoover equations (18) in subsection 2.6 below. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\sigma )$$\end{document}b(σ) is a symplectic automorphism, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }J \mathbin {\cdot }b(\sigma )^* = J$$\end{document}b(σ)·J·b(σ)∗=J, so the equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{y}$$\end{document}y˙ (13a) assumes the form of a Hamiltonian vector field and a friction-like term–\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \varphi (\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }y/\sqrt{R(\sigma )}$$\end{document}ζφ(σ)·y/R(σ)–where the coefficient of friction depends on the thermostat state through the variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma , \zeta $$\end{document}σ,ζ. Also, note that in the equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\zeta }$$\end{document}ζ˙ (13c), the first term–\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textrm{d}{H}(y)},{\sigma \mathbin {\cdot }\varphi (\sigma )\mathbin {\cdot }y}\rangle $$\end{document}⟨dH(y),σ·φ(σ)·y⟩–has the form of an instantaneous temperature. Indeed, beyond the analogy with the like terms in the Nosé–Hoover equations, let’s note that the time rate of change of the internal energy of the system is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{H} = - \dfrac{\zeta }{\sigma \sqrt{R(\sigma )}} \ \langle {\textrm{d}{H}(y)},{\sigma \varphi (\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }y}\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H˙=-ζσR(σ)⟨dH(y),σφ(σ)·y⟩.In subsection 2.6 it is shown that there is a “natural” rescaling of time by the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\sigma ) = \sigma \sqrt{R(\sigma )}$$\end{document}w(σ)=σR(σ). Let’s take that as given. This suggests the interpretation that, in units where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$gk=1$$\end{document}gk=1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H' = - \underbrace{\zeta }_{\mathrm {friction\ coefficient}} \hspace{2.5mm}\times \hspace{2.5mm} \underbrace{\langle {\textrm{d}{H}(y)},{\sigma \varphi (\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }y}\rangle }_{\mathrm {instantaneous\ temperature}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H′=-ζ⏟frictioncoefficient×⟨dH(y),σφ(σ)·y⟩⏟instantaneoustemperature.

Time rescaling

As stressed by Jellinek, the rescaling of time can be chosen independent of the already-mentioned functional parameters in the thermostat, provided that one is willing to use a weighted microcanonical measure. A weighted microcanonical measure is of the form14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}_{T,w}(\psi ) = \dfrac{1}{Z} \times \int \textrm{d}{x}\, \textrm{d}{s}\, \textrm{d}{p_s}\, \psi (a(s) \mathbin {\cdot }x) \times \delta (G-f)/w, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ET,w(ψ)=1Z×∫dxdsdpsψ(a(s)·x)×δ(G-f)/w,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G is the Jellinek–Berry thermostated Hamiltonian (4), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f$$\end{document}f is a constant and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w = w(x,s,p_{s}) > 0$$\end{document}w=w(x,s,ps)>0 is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ function. Jellinek and Jellinek & Berry focus on the case when the weighting function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document}w is a function only of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s$$\end{document}s [9, eqn. 27]; in this subsection, we provide a family of examples that depend non-trivially on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,s,p_s)$$\end{document}(x,s,ps). In the rescaled time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d/d\tau = w \times d/dt$$\end{document}d/dτ=w×d/dt, the weighted microcanonical measure is invariant.

As an example of a weighting function to reproduce the canonical ensemble of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H, Jellinek chooses a weight function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(s) = s^l$$\end{document}w(s)=sl for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l \in {\textbf{R}}$$\end{document}l∈R [9, eqn. 31]. Let’s consider a weight function15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w = \dfrac{\sqrt{R(\sigma )}}{\det (b(\sigma )) \times \sigma ^l} \times \exp \left( a\beta (H(y)+\frac{1}{2} \zeta ^2) \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}w=R(σ)det(b(σ))×σl×expaβ(H(y)+12ζ2),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,l$$\end{document}a,l are arbitrary parameters. By routine calculations, similar to [9, p. 3167], one obtains that the expected value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi : T^* M \rightarrow {\textbf{R}}$$\end{document}ψ:T∗M→R is:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}_{T,w}(\psi )&= \dfrac{1}{Z} \times \int \textrm{d}{x}\, \textrm{d}{s}\, \textrm{d}{p_s}\, \psi (a(s) \mathbin {\cdot }x) \times \delta (G-f)/w \nonumber \\&= \dfrac{1}{Z} \times \int \textrm{d}{y}\,\textrm{d}{\zeta }\,\psi (y)\,\sigma ^l\,\exp (-a\beta (H(y)+\frac{1}{2}\zeta ^2))\,\dfrac{\sigma }{gkT}, \nonumber \\&\qquad {\text {where }}\, gkT \ln \sigma = f - H(y) - \frac{1}{2}\zeta ^2 \nonumber \\&= \dfrac{\sqrt{2\pi \beta }}{gZ} \times e^{\frac{l+1}{g}\beta f} \times \int \textrm{d}{y}\, \psi (y) \times \exp \left( -r \beta H(y)\right) , \nonumber \\&\qquad \mathrm {and\ } r = \frac{l+1}{g}+a, \beta = \frac{1}{kT}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ET,w(ψ)=1Z×∫dxdsdpsψ(a(s)·x)×δ(G-f)/w=1Z×∫dydζψ(y)σlexp(-aβ(H(y)+12ζ2))σgkT,wheregkTlnσ=f-H(y)-12ζ2=2πβgZ×el+1gβf×∫dyψ(y)×exp-rβH(y),andr=l+1g+a,β=1kT.Since the remaining integral should compute the expected value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi $$\end{document}ψ in the canonical ensemble of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H at temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, one sees that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r$$\end{document}r must be unity. The normalization constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z$$\end{document}Z is chosen to ensure the expected value of unity is unity. By inspection, one obtains17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g&= \dfrac{l+1}{1-a} & \textrm{and}&Z&= \dfrac{(1-a)\sqrt{2\pi \beta }}{l+1} \times e^{(1-a)\beta f} \times Z_0(\beta ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g=l+11-aandZ=(1-a)2πβl+1×e(1-a)βf×Z0(β)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_0(\beta )$$\end{document}Z0(β) is the partition function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H, which equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \textrm{d}{y}\, e^{-\beta H(y)} $$\end{document}∫dye-βH(y).

The Hoover reduction

Hoover shows how to reduce the Hamiltonian equations of the Nosé thermostated Hamiltonian by means of a change of state variable and re-parameterization of time [7, eqn. 6], c.f. [14, eqns. 2.19–22]. In the notation of subsection 2.4, the Nosé–Hoover thermostat equations take the form18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} y' = J \mathbin {\cdot }\textrm{d}{H}(y) - \frac{\zeta }{Q} \xi \mathbin {\cdot }y, \qquad \zeta ' = \langle {\textrm{d}{H}(y)},{\xi \mathbin {\cdot }y}\rangle - gkT, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}y′=J·dH(y)-ζQξ·y,ζ′=⟨dH(y),ξ·y⟩-gkT,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q$$\end{document}Q is the thermostat mass from (2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ projects the state vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=(q,p)$$\end{document}y=(q,p) onto the momentum vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p$$\end{document}p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f'$$\end{document}f′ is the derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f$$\end{document}f with respect to a rescaled time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} These equations are obtained by a time-recaling and reduction of the Hamiltonian differential equations of the Nosé Hamiltonian (2).

Let’s say that a transformation of state and re-parameterization of time is a Hoover reduction of the Jellinek–Berry thermostated equations if it transforms the differential equations (13) into a form in which the thermostat state variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ is redundant and at any critical point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=y_0$$\end{document}y=y0 of the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}H, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta ' = -gkT$$\end{document}ζ′=-gkT. The first condition ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ can be eliminated from the differential equations, while the second condition fixes the rescaling of time.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \varphi (\sigma ) = \xi $$\end{document}σφ(σ)=ξ, a constant element in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{gl}({{\textbf{R}}^{2n}})$$\end{document}gl(R2n), the vector space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2n \times 2n$$\end{document}2n×2n real matrices;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\sigma ) \times b(\sigma ) \mathbin {\cdot }J \mathbin {\cdot }b(\sigma )^* = \Lambda $$\end{document}w(σ)×b(σ)·J·b(σ)∗=Λ, a fixed skew symmetric matrix that is congruent to the symplectic matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J$$\end{document}J;

C.The following conditions hold: C.4.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\sigma ) = \exp (-\ln (\sigma )\xi )\mathbin {\cdot }b(1)$$\end{document}b(σ)=exp(-ln(σ)ξ)·b(1);C.5.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\sigma ) = w(1) \sigma ^{2\kappa }$$\end{document}w(σ)=w(1)σ2κ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = \frac{1}{2n} \operatorname {tr}(\xi )$$\end{document}κ=12ntr(ξ);C.6.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = b(1)^{-1} \mathbin {\cdot }(\xi - \kappa 1) \mathbin {\cdot }b(1)$$\end{document}η=b(1)-1·(ξ-κ1)·b(1) is an infinitesimally symplectic matrix;C.7.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(\sigma ) = R(1) \sigma ^{4\kappa -2}$$\end{document}R(σ)=R(1)σ4κ-2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(1)=w(1)^2$$\end{document}R(1)=w(1)2;C.8.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(1)^{-1} \mathbin {\cdot }b(\sigma ) = \sigma ^{-\kappa } \times \exp (-\ln (\sigma )\eta )$$\end{document}b(1)-1·b(σ)=σ-κ×exp(-ln(σ)η) is a conformally symplectic group.

The following conditions hold: C.4.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\sigma ) = \exp (-\ln (\sigma )\xi )\mathbin {\cdot }b(1)$$\end{document}b(σ)=exp(-ln(σ)ξ)·b(1);C.5.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\sigma ) = w(1) \sigma ^{2\kappa }$$\end{document}w(σ)=w(1)σ2κ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = \frac{1}{2n} \operatorname {tr}(\xi )$$\end{document}κ=12ntr(ξ);C.6.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = b(1)^{-1} \mathbin {\cdot }(\xi -
