From (44), it follows that the orbits of the Jellinek–Berry thermostated Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G (43) are, up to a temperature-dependent rescaling of time, the orbits of a Jellinek–Berry thermostated Hamiltonian with potential energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta V$$\end{document}βV with temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=1/gk$$\end{document}T=1/gk. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 0$$\end{document}β=0, the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{1,0}$$\end{document}G1,0 is the Jellinek–Berry thermostated ideal gas Hamiltonian (28). In theorem 5 it is proven that this Hamiltonian’s frequency map is R-non-degenerate. Rüssmann proves that if a real-analytically integrable Hamiltonian has a frequency map that is R-non-degenerate, then, for all sufficiently small perturbations of the Hamiltonian, a positive-measure set of invariant tori survive the perturbation [17, p. 11], [16, p. 126].

On the other hand, in Propositon 6, it is proven that if the functional parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(r,b)$$\end{document}(r,b) lie in an open and dense set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ functional parameters, then the frequency map of the Jellinek–Berry thermostated ideal gas Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G (28) is Kolmogorov non-degenerate. Kolmogorov (and Arnol’d) prove that, given real-analytic data, a positive-measure set of invariant tori survive perturbation; Salamon proves that the same conclusions hold if the data are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ in action-angle variables with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell > 2N$$\end{document}ℓ>2N where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N$$\end{document}N is the number of degrees of freedom (or, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell > 2N+1$$\end{document}ℓ>2N+1 in “physical” coordinates) [18, p. 24, Theorem 2].

This discussion proves the following theorem (the main result of this note):

Theorem 8

Assume that the Hamiltonian (43) is either real-analytic, or it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\ell }$$\end{document}Cℓ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell > 2n+3$$\end{document}ℓ>2n+3, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(r,b)$$\end{document}(r,b) are in general position. Then, there is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0 > 0$$\end{document}T0>0 such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>T_0$$\end{document}T>T0, then the Hamiltonian (43) has a positive measure set of invariant tori. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}T→∞, the Lebesgue density of that set tends to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1$$\end{document}1.

It is well-known that such positive-measure sets of invariant tori prevent ergodicity of the Hamiltonian flow.

Conclusion

This note studies the Jellinek–Berry thermostat. It shows that, in the typical case, the thermostated Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G$$\end{document}G, (4), does not admit a reduction similar to the Hoover reduction of the Nosé thermostat. Despite this, the thermostated ideal gas is always completely integrable, the Hamiltonian’s frequency map satisfies Rüssmann’s non-degeneracy condition and the typical one also satisfies Kolmogorov’s non-degeneracy condition. Each of these conditions imply that for suitable perturbations, a majority of invariant tori survive. This includes the case where the ideal-gas Hamiltonian is perturbed by a sufficiently smooth potential energy. Somewhat paradoxically, a sufficiently high temperature of the heat bath increases the measure of the set of invariant tori.

The thermostated ideal-gas Hamiltonian is the simplest example to consider in this family of thermostated examples. It is natural to ask what happens when one turns to the thermostated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document}n-degree of freedom harmonic oscillator:45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G(x,\nu ,p_{\nu }) = \frac{1}{2} \langle {c(\nu ) \mathbin {\cdot }x},{x}\rangle + \frac{1}{2} r(\nu ) p_{\nu }^2 + gkT \nu . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G(x,ν,pν)=12⟨c(ν)·x,x⟩+12r(ν)pν2+gkTν.As above, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = (q,p)$$\end{document}x=(q,p) is the state variable of the harmonic oscillator and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\nu ) = b(\nu )^* \mathbin {\cdot }b(\nu )$$\end{document}c(ν)=b(ν)∗·b(ν) is the symmetric, positive-definite quadratic form induced by the coupling matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(\nu )$$\end{document}b(ν). The presence of a non-trivial potential energy significantly complicates the analysis of this Hamiltonian compared to the periodic ideal gas. For example, although it is known that the Nosé thermostated 1-dimensional harmonic oscillator is not ergodic [13], it is unknown if that is the case for higher dimensions. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, the Nosé thermostated system with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n$$\end{document}n identical oscillators does have linear-in-momenta first integrals due to its invariance under the action of SO(n) [12, Theorem 3.1], [5, Proposition 1]. It is not known if the system is ergodic on a fixed level of these angular momentum first integrals, however.

It is well-known that a perturbation of the harmonic oscillator can destroy all invariant tori via the well-known Anosov–Katok construction [1, 2]. On the other hand, if the Jellinek–Berry coupling form, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\nu )$$\end{document}c(ν), is sufficiently anisotropic, then one might expect that there are parameter regimes for the Hamiltonian (45) in which it decomposes into a non-degenerate integrable Hamiltonian plus a small perturbation.
